package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/**
 * All records of database 1: languages, systems, devices, tools and practices of the 1980s,
 * as well as guides, examples, projects, flex projects and cross-code experiments.
 * The display order is set by the screen (kind → section → year), not by the order in these lists.
 */
val db1Records: List<RecordEntity> =
    db1Languages + db1Systems + db1Devices + db1Tools + db1Practices +
        db1Guides + db1Examples + db1Projects + db1Flex + db1Cross + db1Additions

################################################################################
# FILE: RetroDatabases/app/src/main/java/com/example/retrodatabases/data/seed/Db1Cross.kt
################################################################################
package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.Kind
import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/**
 * Cross-code experiments: technologies and code are mixed for the sake of the experiment.
 * All these mixes share one interface: a text stream, a file or build rules.
 */
val db1Cross: List<RecordEntity> = listOf(

    entry(
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Filters and pipelines",
        title = "A C filter in a pipeline with awk and sed",
        originalTitle = "C filter in an awk and sed pipeline",
        author = "Unix practice, 1978",
        year = 1978,
        summary = "awk prepares the data, C computes fast, sed formats the output: three languages in one pipeline with no interfaces between them.",
        details = "Each program reads standard input and writes standard output, so the boundary between the languages is a pipe. " +
            "Heavy computation is written in C, data preparation in awk, and formatting in sed. " +
            "The experiment shows that technologies can be mixed if they share an interface: text.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the C filter as square.c and build it: cc -o square square.c
            2. Run the pipeline from the example.
            3. The result: the squares 1, 4, 9, 16 and 25, each with the prefix “square: ”.
        """,
        example = """
            ### square.c
            #include <stdio.h>

            int main(void)
            {
                long n;

                while (scanf("%ld", &n) == 1)
                    printf("%ld\n", n * n);
                return 0;
            }

            ### pipeline (in the shell)
            awk 'BEGIN { for (i = 1; i <= 5; i++) print i }' | ./square | sed 's/^/square: /'
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Code generation",
        title = "Sine table at build time: make, awk and C",
        originalTitle = "Build-time code generation with make, awk and C",
        author = "make practice, 1976",
        year = 1976,
        summary = "make runs awk, which writes a table in C, and only then builds the program that uses it.",
        details = "The table is computed at build time, not while the program runs: a trick of the 80s, when every clock cycle was expensive. " +
            "The Makefile describes the dependencies: sine.h depends on gen.awk, and the program depends on sine.h. " +
            "Change the generator and everything that depends on it is rebuilt. This is how build rules tie different languages together.",
        instructions = """
            1. Create the files gen.awk, Makefile and main.c from the blocks in the example. Command lines in the Makefile start with a tab.
            2. Run make: sine.h and the program prog appear.
            3. Run ./prog — it prints the sine values for eight angles from 0 to 7π/4.
        """,
        example = """
            ### gen.awk
            BEGIN {
                print "static const double sine_table[8] = {"
                for (i = 0; i < 8; i++)
                    printf "    %.6f,\n", sin(i * 3.14159265358979 / 4)
                print "};"
            }

            ### Makefile
            prog: main.c sine.h
            <TAB>cc -o prog main.c

            sine.h: gen.awk
            <TAB>awk -f gen.awk > sine.h

            ### main.c
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include "sine.h"

            int main(void)
            {
                int i;

                for (i = 0; i < 8; i++)
                    printf("%d: %.6f\n", i, sine_table[i]);
                return 0;
            }
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Logs and reports",
        title = "Log analysis pipeline: perl, sed, sort",
        originalTitle = "Log analysis with perl, sed, sort and uniq",
        author = "Perl and Unix practice",
        year = 1987,
        summary = "Perl picks fields from the log, sort and uniq count, sed formats the output: each tool does what it does best.",
        details = "In -n mode, Perl reads the log line by line and prints the first field, the address. " +
            "Then sort and uniq -c count the repeats, sort -rn builds the ranking, and sed removes the extra spaces. " +
            "The experiment shows that you do not have to pick one language for everything.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the sample log as access.log. The first field of each line is an address:
               10.0.0.5 GET /index.html
               10.0.0.7 GET /about.html
               10.0.0.5 GET /c.html
               10.0.0.9 GET /index.html
               10.0.0.5 GET /awk.html
               10.0.0.7 GET /perl.html
            2. Run the command from the example.
            3. The result: 3 10.0.0.5, then 2 10.0.0.7 and 1 10.0.0.9.
        """,
        example = """
            perl -ne 'print "$1\n" if /^(\S+)/' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | sed 's/^ *//' | head -3
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Languages inside languages",
        title = "Mini Forth in C: a stack inside another language",
        originalTitle = "A mini Forth interpreter in C",
        author = "An experiment in the spirit of Charles Moore",
        year = 1970,
        summary = "The stack model of Forth is implemented in C: numbers go onto the stack, operator words take their arguments from it.",
        details = "The interpreter is an array-stack and a loop over the words: a number is pushed onto the stack, the word + or * pops two values " +
            "and pushes the result. This way a language inside a language takes a few dozen lines. " +
            "The experiment shows how any stack-based language works, PostScript included.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as mini4th.c and build it: cc -o mini4th mini4th.c
            2. Run: echo '2 3 + 4 * .' | ./mini4th — the answer is 20.
            3. Try: echo '10 2 - .' | ./mini4th — the answer is 8.
        """,
        example = """
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include <stdlib.h>
            #include <string.h>

            #define STACK_MAX 64

            static long stack[STACK_MAX];
            static int sp = 0;                  /* number of elements on the stack */

            static void push(long v)
            {
                if (sp >= STACK_MAX) {
                    fprintf(stderr, "stack overflow\n");
                    exit(1);
                }
                stack[sp++] = v;
            }

            static long pop(void)
            {
                if (sp <= 0) {
                    fprintf(stderr, "stack underflow\n");
                    exit(1);
                }
                return stack[--sp];
            }

            int main(void)
            {
                char word[64];

                while (scanf("%63s", word) == 1) {
                    if (strcmp(word, "+") == 0) {
                        long b = pop();
                        push(pop() + b);
                    } else if (strcmp(word, "-") == 0) {
                        long b = pop();
                        push(pop() - b);
                    } else if (strcmp(word, "*") == 0) {
                        long b = pop();
                        push(pop() * b);
                    } else if (strcmp(word, ".") == 0) {
                        printf("%ld\n", pop());
                    } else {
                        push(strtol(word, NULL, 10));   /* any other word is a number */
                    }
                }
                return 0;
            }
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Computation",
        title = "π to sixty digits: bc and awk",
        originalTitle = "Pi to 60 digits with bc and awk",
        author = "bc: Lorinda Cherry and Robert Morris, Bell Labs, 1975",
        year = 1975,
        summary = "The arbitrary-precision calculator bc computes π, and awk prints the result with a label.",
        details = "The function a(1) in bc gives arctan(1), and multiplying it by 4 gives π. The precision is set by the variable scale. " +
            "bc works with numbers of any length, so π can be computed to any number of digits. " +
            "awk takes the result from the pipeline and prints it with a label.",
        instructions = """
            1. Run the command from the example.
            2. The result starts like this: pi = 3.141592653589793238462643383279502884197169399375105820974944
        """,
        example = """
            echo "scale=60; 4*a(1)" | bc -l | awk '{ print "pi =", $1 }'
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),
)

################################################################################
# FILE: RetroDatabases/app/src/main/java/com/example/retrodatabases/data/seed/Db1Examples.kt
################################################################################
package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.Kind
import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/** Usage examples: short recipes that solve real problems of the 80s. */
val db1Examples: List<RecordEntity> = listOf(

    entry(
        kind = Kind.EXAMPLE,
        section = "Text data",
        title = "Word frequency in a text",
        originalTitle = "Word frequency with tr, sort, uniq",
        author = "Unix practice",
        year = 1973,
        summary = "One line of shell tells us which words occur most often in a text.",
        details = "tr replaces everything that is not a letter with a newline, so each word ends up on its own line. " +
            "sort puts identical words next to each other, uniq -c counts the repeats, and the last sort -rn lists the most frequent words at the top. " +
            "This is how people analysed documentation and texts in the 80s.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save any text as book.txt.
            2. Run the command from the example.
            3. The output shows the count on the left and the word on the right; the most frequent words are at the top.
        """,
        example = """
            tr -cs 'A-Za-z' '\n' < book.txt | tr 'A-Z' 'a-z' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.EXAMPLE,
        section = "Text data",
        title = "Bulk replacement in a text: sed",
        originalTitle = "sed substitution and line selection",
        author = "Lee McMahon, Bell Labs",
        year = 1974,
        summary = "Replacing a word on every line of a file without opening an editor.",
        details = "The command s/pattern/replacement/g changes every occurrence on each line; the g flag means “all”, not just the first one. " +
            "Addresses such as 2,4 select lines, and /pattern/d deletes the matching lines. " +
            "It is convenient to send the result to a new file, so the original text stays intact.",
        instructions = """
            1. Create notes.txt with several lines, one of which contains the word Unix.
            2. Run the three commands from the example one after another.
            3. To keep the result, add > notes-new.txt to the command.
        """,
        example = """
            sed 's/Unix/UNIX/g' notes.txt
            sed -n '2,4p' notes.txt
            sed '/^#/d' notes.txt
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.EXAMPLE,
        section = "Logs and reports",
        title = "Top errors in a log",
        originalTitle = "Error report with grep, cut, sort, uniq",
        author = "Unix practice",
        year = 1973,
        summary = "We pull the error lines out of a long log and count which codes occur most often.",
        details = "grep selects the lines with ERROR, cut extracts the third field, sort and uniq -c count the repeats, and sort -rn prints the ranking. " +
            "This is how reports were built in the 80s without programming: the pipeline ran on any Unix machine and needed no compiler.",
        instructions = """
            1. Create a file errors.log with this content:
               12:00 ERROR E42 disk full
               12:01 INFO start
               12:02 ERROR E17 no paper
               12:03 ERROR E42 disk full
               12:04 ERROR E42 disk full
            2. Run the command from the example.
            3. The result: E42 appears 3 times, E17 once.
        """,
        example = """
            grep 'ERROR' errors.log | cut -d' ' -f3 | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -3
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.EXAMPLE,
        section = "C programs",
        title = "Sorting any array: qsort",
        originalTitle = "qsort from the C standard library",
        author = "C standard library",
        year = 1979,
        summary = "One function sorts arrays of any type: the comparison rule is passed as a pointer to a function.",
        details = "qsort takes an array, the number of elements, the size of an element and a comparison function. " +
            "The same code sorts numbers, strings and records; this was an important trick of portable libraries in the 80s. " +
            "The comparison function returns a negative, zero or positive value.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as sortdemo.c.
            2. Build it: cc -o sortdemo sortdemo.c
            3. Run ./sortdemo — the numbers are printed in ascending order: 1, 3, 7, 19, 42, 88.
        """,
        example = """
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include <stdlib.h>

            static int compare(const void *a, const void *b)
            {
                int x = *(const int *)a;
                int y = *(const int *)b;
                return (x > y) - (x < y);
            }

            int main(void)
            {
                int values[] = { 42, 7, 19, 3, 88, 1 };
                size_t n = sizeof values / sizeof values[0];
                size_t i;

                qsort(values, n, sizeof values[0], compare);
                for (i = 0; i < n; i++)
                    printf("%d\n", values[i]);
                return 0;
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.EXAMPLE,
        section = "Databases",
        title = "Summary report: GROUP BY",
        originalTitle = "SQL GROUP BY report",
        author = "IBM, SEQUEL language",
        year = 1974,
        summary = "GROUP BY turns records into a summary: how many there are and of which kinds.",
        details = "The aggregate functions COUNT, MIN, MAX and SUM work over groups of rows that share the same field value. " +
            "Such reports used to be written as separate COBOL programs; SQL does the same in one query.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the example as report.sql.
            2. Run it in any SQL engine, for example sqlite3: sqlite3 < report.sql
            3. The result: for each kind, the number of records and the earliest year.
        """,
        example = """
            CREATE TABLE records (kind TEXT, title TEXT, year INTEGER);
            INSERT INTO records VALUES ('LANGUAGE', 'Pascal', 1970);
            INSERT INTO records VALUES ('LANGUAGE', 'C', 1972);
            INSERT INTO records VALUES ('TOOL', 'make', 1976);

            SELECT kind, COUNT(*) AS total, MIN(year) AS first_year
            FROM records
            GROUP BY kind
            ORDER BY total DESC;
        """,
    ),
)

package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.Kind
import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/** Practices of the 1980s that were missing from the first edition: design, formal methods, standards and culture. */
val db1ExtraPractices: List<RecordEntity> = listOf(

    // ---------- Design ----------

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "Abstract data types",
        originalTitle = "Abstract data types",
        author = "Barbara Liskov, Stephen Zilles",
        year = 1974,
        summary = "A type is described by a set of operations rather than by its data layout; the internal representation is hidden.",
        details = "Liskov and Zilles (1974) proposed describing types through behaviour. This idea became the basis of OOP and of modern libraries.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "Liskov substitution principle",
        originalTitle = "Liskov substitution principle",
        author = "Barbara Liskov",
        year = 1987,
        summary = "A subclass must work wherever its base class works, without breaking the program's expectations.",
        details = "The principle was formulated in 1987. It explains when inheritance is safe, and it underlies the design of interfaces.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "Design patterns",
        originalTitle = "Design patterns",
        author = "Kent Beck, Ward Cunningham",
        year = 1987,
        summary = "Reusable solutions to typical problems in object-oriented programs: the seed of the future Gang of Four book.",
        details = "Beck and Cunningham presented patterns at OOPSLA 1987 in the context of Smalltalk. The ideas were later collected in a 1994 book.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "CRC cards",
        originalTitle = "CRC cards (Class-Responsibility-Collaboration)",
        author = "Kent Beck, Ward Cunningham",
        year = 1989,
        summary = "Designing classes on index cards: the class, its responsibilities and its collaborators.",
        details = "The cards let a group design objects without a computer. The method is handy for teaching object-oriented thinking.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "Jackson's method (JSP)",
        originalTitle = "Jackson structured programming",
        author = "Michael Jackson",
        year = 1975,
        summary = "A program's structure is derived from the structure of its input and output data.",
        details = "Jackson (1975) proposed building a program from the hierarchy of its data. The method was widely used in Britain for business systems.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "Warnier–Orr diagrams",
        originalTitle = "Warnier–Orr diagrams",
        author = "Jean-Dominique Warnier, Kenneth Orr",
        year = 1974,
        summary = "Nested brackets describe the structure of data and of a program hierarchically: from the general to the specific.",
        details = "Warnier proposed the diagrams in 1974, and Orr developed them in 1977. The diagrams were used to design reports and files.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "HIPO diagrams",
        originalTitle = "HIPO (Hierarchy plus Input-Process-Output)",
        author = "IBM",
        year = 1974,
        summary = "A hierarchy of modules with a description of the inputs, processing and outputs of each part of the program.",
        details = "HIPO was created at IBM to document large systems. Each block described its input, process and output separately.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "Jackson System Development",
        originalTitle = "JSD (Jackson System Development)",
        author = "Michael Jackson",
        year = 1983,
        summary = "A method of modelling a system by its events, with the life cycle of each object.",

            "The method was used in banking systems.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",
        title = "Information Engineering",
        originalTitle = "Information Engineering",
        author = "Clive Finkelstein, James Martin",
        year = 1981,
        summary = "A methodology (1981) for designing a company's whole information system from its data and processes.",
        details = "The methodology combined data and process models into one picture. It was used for large corporate systems.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",

        summary = "Development in short iterations with prototypes and tools that speeded up building screens.",

    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Design",

        author = "Grady Booch",
        year = 1986,
        summary = "Object-oriented design (1986): class and object diagrams for large systems.",
        details = "Booch described the method in a 1986 book. His diagrams became the basis of the UML notations that appeared later.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    // ---------- Quality and testing ----------

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Quality and testing",
        title = "Z notation",
        originalTitle = "Z notation",
        author = "Jean-Raymond Abrial, Oxford",
        year = 1980,
        summary = "A formal specification (1980) in mathematical notation: requirements are written as predicates.",
        details = "The Z notation was used in industrial specifications, including for security systems. It combines set theory and first-order logic.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Quality and testing",
        title = "VDM method",
        originalTitle = "Vienna Development Method (VDM)",
        author = "Dines Bjørner, Cliff Jones",
        year = 1978,
        summary = "A formal specification method (1978) from Vienna: data models and operations with preconditions.",
        details = "VDM combined specification and correctness proofs through refinement steps. The method was used in industrial projects.",
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Quality and testing",
        title = "Mutation testing",
        originalTitle = "Mutation testing",
        author = "Richard DeMillo, Richard Lipton, Frederick Sayward",

################################################################################
# FILE: RetroDatabases/app/src/main/java/com/example/retrodatabases/data/seed/Db1Flex.kt
################################################################################
package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.Kind
import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/**
 * Flex projects: the maximum is squeezed out of minimal code, just to show what is possible.
 * Each record shows which trick is pushed to the limit and why it works.
 */
val db1Flex: List<RecordEntity> = listOf(

    entry(
        kind = Kind.FLEX,
        section = "Text graphics",
        title = "Sierpinski triangle from one bitwise AND",
        originalTitle = "Sierpinski pattern with x & y",
        author = "Demoscene practice and C books",
        year = 1985,
        summary = "A single bitwise operator draws a fractal: an asterisk goes where the bits of the coordinates do not overlap.",
        details = "The condition (x & y) == 0 gives a pattern similar to the Sierpinski triangle: these are Pascal's triangle cells modulo 2. " +
            "The program has no geometric formulas at all, only a loop over the coordinates and a bitwise AND. " +
            "One-liners like this were a favorite in the demoscene and in C books.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as sierpinski.c and build it: cc -o sierpinski sierpinski.c
            2. Run ./sierpinski — the screen shows 32 lines of the fractal.
            3. To see more levels, replace both 32s with 64.
        """,
        example = """
            #include <stdio.h>

            int main(void)
            {
                int x, y;

                for (y = 0; y < 32; y++) {
                    for (x = 0; x < 32; x++)
                        putchar((x & y) ? ' ' : '*');
                    putchar('\n');
                }
                return 0;
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.FLEX,
        section = "Self-reproduction",
        title = "Quine: a program that prints its own code",
        originalTitle = "C quine",
        author = "Classic C quines, 1980s",
        year = 1984,
        summary = "A program with no input prints its own source text exactly.",
        details = "The string holds a template of the whole program. printf puts the program's own text into the template, and the numbers 34 are the codes of the double quote, so the quote does not need escaping. " +
            "This is how the program knows itself through its own string. " +
            "The same trick lies behind self-reproducing compilers, which Ken Thompson described in his lecture “Reflections on Trusting Trust” (1984).",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the program's line to a file quine.c (with a newline at the end).
            2. Build in the old C mode: cc -std=gnu89 -w -o quine quine.c
            3. Run ./quine > out.c and compare: diff quine.c out.c. The only difference is the newline at the end of the file.
        """,
        example = """
            main(){char*c="main(){char*c=%c%s%c;printf(c,34,c,34);}";printf(c,34,c,34);}
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.FLEX,
        section = "Text graphics",
        title = "Mandelbrot set in awk",
        originalTitle = "Mandelbrot set in awk",
        author = "Benoit Mandelbrot (fractals, 1980)",
        year = 1980,
        summary = "A fractal in the terminal: floating-point arithmetic and a character scale right inside a scripting language.",
        details = "For every point c of the plane we repeat z = z² + c and check whether |z| leaves the circle of radius 2. " +
            "If it has not left within the given number of steps, the point belongs to the set and is printed as “#”. " +
            "The other characters show how fast the point escaped. " +
            "The whole “renderer” is two nested loops and a string scale.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as mandel.awk.
            2. Run: awk -f mandel.awk
            3. To zoom in on a fragment, reduce the ranges of x and y, keeping the step.
        """,
        example = """
            BEGIN {
                ramp = " .:-=+*"                  # characters by escape speed
                for (y = -1.2; y <= 1.2; y += 0.1) {
                    line = ""
                    for (x = -2.1; x <= 0.6; x += 0.04) {
                        zr = 0; zi = 0; n = 0
                        while (zr * zr + zi * zi < 4 && n < 30) {
                            t = zr * zr - zi * zi + x
                            zi = 2 * zr * zi + y
                            zr = t
                            n++
                        }
                        if (n == 30)
                            c = "#"
                        else
                            c = substr(ramp, int(n / 5) + 1, 1)
                        line = line c
                    }
                    print line
                }
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.FLEX,
        section = "One-liners",
        title = "Run-length compression in one awk line",
        originalTitle = "Run-length encoding in one awk line",
        author = "Compression practice, 1980s",
        year = 1985,
        summary = "Repeated characters are written as “count and character”; all the logic fits on one awk line.",
        details = "The run-length encoding scheme was used in graphics formats of the 80s, for example in PCX (1985). " +
            "The outer loop moves along the string, and the inner one counts the length of a run. " +
            "The reverse operation, decompression, is an exercise for the reader.",
        instructions = """
            1. Run the command from the example.
            2. For the string aaabccdddd the result is 3a1b2c4d.
            3. Try a string without repeats, for example abcdef: the result is longer than the input. That is why RLE only helps on repeated data.
        """,
        example = """
            echo aaabccdddd | awk '{ s = $0; o = ""; i = 1; while (i <= length(s)) { c = substr(s, i, 1); r = 1; while (substr(s, i + r, 1) == c) r++; o = o r c; i += r } print o }'
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.FLEX,
        section = "One-liners",
        title = "Letter frequencies with a Unix pipeline",
        originalTitle = "Letter frequency pipeline",
        author = "Cryptanalysis practice, 1980s",
        year = 1980,
        summary = "Five Unix tools count letter frequencies in a text: the basis of frequency analysis in cryptanalysis.",
        details = "tr removes everything except Latin letters and lowercases them. fold -w1 splits the text into one character per line, " +
            "sort and uniq -c count the repeats, and sort -rn puts the most frequent letters on top. " +
            "In English text E, T and A lead the list. Frequency tables like this one are used to find the key letters of a cipher.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save an English text as text.txt.
            2. Run the command from the example.
            3. The output shows the count and the letter. Compare them with the letter-frequency table for the language.
        """,
        example = """
            tr -cd 'A-Za-z' < text.txt | tr 'A-Z' 'a-z' | fold -w1 | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5
        """,
    ),
)

# FILE: RetroDatabases/app/src/main/java/com/example/retrodatabases/data/seed/Db1Guides.kt

package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.Kind
import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/** Guides and instructions: how the techniques of the 1980s work and how to repeat them by hand. */
val db1Guides: List<RecordEntity> = listOf(

    entry(
        kind = Kind.GUIDE,
        section = "How it works",
        title = "How a Unix pipeline works",
        originalTitle = "How pipes work",
        author = "Unix practice, Bell Labs",
        year = 1973,
        summary = "A pipeline is two processes connected by a kernel buffer: the output of one becomes the input of the other.",
        details = "The shell creates a channel with pipe(), starts processes with fork() and redirects the standard streams with dup2(). " +
            "The reader blocks until the writer puts data into the channel; when the writer closes its end, the reader gets end-of-file. " +
            "This is how the command ls | wc -l works without temporary files, and each program does not know what it is connected to.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the example as pipeline.c and build it: cc -o pipeline pipeline.c
            2. Run: ./pipeline
            3. The program does what the shell does for the command echo hello | tr a-z A-Z, and prints HELLO.
        """,
        example = """
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include <stdlib.h>
            #include <unistd.h>
            #include <sys/wait.h>

            int main(void)
            {
                int fd[2];

                if (pipe(fd) == -1) {
                    perror("pipe");
                    return 1;
                }

                if (fork() == 0) {                  /* first process: echo writes into the pipe */
                    dup2(fd[1], STDOUT_FILENO);
                    close(fd[0]);
                    close(fd[1]);
                    execlp("echo", "echo", "hello", (char *)NULL);
                    perror("execlp echo");
                    exit(1);
                }

                if (fork() == 0) {                  /* second process: tr reads from the pipe */
                    dup2(fd[0], STDIN_FILENO);
                    close(fd[0]);
                    close(fd[1]);
                    execlp("tr", "tr", "a-z", "A-Z", (char *)NULL);
                    perror("execlp tr");
                    exit(1);
                }

                close(fd[0]);                       /* the parent needs neither end of the pipe */
                close(fd[1]);
                wait(NULL);
                wait(NULL);
                return 0;
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.GUIDE,
        section = "Step-by-step instructions",
        title = "Makefile from scratch: step by step",
        originalTitle = "Makefile step by step",
        author = "Stuart Feldman (make), 80s practice",
        year = 1976,
        summary = "Creating a Makefile step by step: targets, dependencies and commands that run only when files change.",
        details = "A rule consists of a target, a list of dependencies and commands. Commands must start with a tab character; " +
            "this is the most common beginner mistake. Make compares the modification time of the target and its dependencies: " +
            "if a dependency is newer, the target is rebuilt. So after editing one file, a rebuild takes seconds, not minutes.",
        instructions = """
            1. Create the files main.c, util.c and util.h with simple functions.
            2. Save the example as Makefile in the same folder. Command lines must start with a tab, not with spaces.
            3. Run make: the first target in the file becomes the default target.
            4. Change util.c and run make again: only util.o and prog are rebuilt.
        """,
        example = """
            prog: main.o util.o
            <TAB>cc -o prog main.o util.o

            main.o: main.c util.h
            <TAB>cc -c main.c

            util.o: util.c util.h
            <TAB>cc -c util.c
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.GUIDE,
        section = "How it works",
        title = "Recursive descent: how to parse expressions",
        originalTitle = "Recursive descent parsing",
        author = "Compiler practice, 1970s–80s",
        year = 1975,
        summary = "Each grammar rule becomes a function. This is how expressions such as 2 + 3 * (4 - 1) are parsed.",
        details = "The grammar: an expression is terms joined by + and -; a term is factors joined by * and /; " +
            "a factor is a number or an expression in parentheses. Each nonterminal corresponds to a function that reads its part of the string " +
            "and returns a value. Operator precedence comes out naturally: multiplication is parsed deeper than addition. " +
            "Most parsers of the 80s were built on this technique.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as calc.c and build it: cc -o calc calc.c
            2. Run: ./calc "2 + 3 * (4 - 1)". The answer is 11.
            3. Check: ./calc "10 / 4" (the answer is 2.5) and ./calc "(1 + 2) * (3 + 4)" (the answer is 21).
        """,
        example = """
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include <stdlib.h>
            #include <ctype.h>

            static const char *p;                   /* current position in the input string */

            static double expr(void);

            static void skip(void)
            {
                while (isspace((unsigned char)*p))
                    p++;
            }

            static double factor(void)              /* factor: a number or (expression) */
            {
                double v;
                char *end;

                skip();
                if (*p == '(') {
                    p++;
                    v = expr();
                    skip();
                    if (*p == ')')
                        p++;
                    return v;
                }
                v = strtod(p, &end);
                p = end;
                return v;
            }

            static double term(void)                /* term: factors joined by * and / */
            {
                double v = factor();

                for (;;) {
                    skip();
                    if (*p == '*') {
                        p++;
                        v *= factor();
                    } else if (*p == '/') {
                        p++;
                        v /= factor();
                    } else {
                        return v;
                    }
                }
            }

            static double expr(void)                /* expression: terms joined by + and - */
            {
                double v = term();

                for (;;) {
                    skip();
                    if (*p == '+') {
                        p++;
                        v += term();
                    } else if (*p == '-') {
                        p++;
                        v -= term();
                    } else {
                        return v;
                    }
                }
            }

            int main(int argc, char *argv[])
            {
                if (argc != 2) {
                    fprintf(stderr, "usage: %s \"expression\"\n", argv[0]);
                    return 1;
                }
                p = argv[1];
                printf("%g\n", expr());
                return 0;
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.GUIDE,
        section = "How it works",
        title = "Forth stack step by step",
        originalTitle = "Forth stack trace",
        author = "Charles Moore",
        year = 1970,
        summary = "Forth executes words from left to right, and the data lives on a stack. A step-by-step trace shows every state.",
        details = "A number pushes its value onto the top of the stack. The word + pops the two top values and pushes their sum. " +
            "The dot “.” pops the top and prints it. A program is a sequence of words, so debugging comes down to one question: " +
            "what is on the stack after this word? Moore used this approach in control systems long before personal computers.",
        instructions = """
            1. Install GNU Forth (the gforth package) or any other Forth interpreter.
            2. Run gforth and type: 2 3 + 4 * .
            3. The output shows 20. Check the table below: after each word the state of the stack changes.
        """,
        example = """
            Word    Stack (bottom left, top right)     Comment
            2       2                                  pushed the number
            3       2 3                                one more number
            +       5                                  popped 2 and 3, pushed 5
            4       5 4                                pushed 4
            *       20                                 popped 5 and 4, pushed 20
            .       (empty)                            popped 20 and printed it
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.GUIDE,
        section = "Step-by-step instructions",
        title = "How to run old programs in an emulator",
        originalTitle = "Running 80s software in emulators",
        author = "Retrocomputing community",
        year = 1982,
        summary = "Old programs run in an emulator if you have the program file, an emulator and the right machine model.",
        details = "An emulator imitates the processor, memory and peripherals. For the ZX Spectrum, Fuse is a good choice; for MS-DOS, DOSBox. " +
            "Programs are distributed as images: .tap and .z80 for the Spectrum, .img and .exe for DOS. " +
            "Many 80s programs were started with the LOAD command and an empty file name, just as on a real machine.",
        instructions = """
            1. Install an emulator: Fuse for the ZX Spectrum or DOSBox for MS-DOS.
            2. Open the program file through the emulator's menu (File → Open).
            3. In the Spectrum emulator, type LOAD "" (the keyword LOAD and two quotes), press Enter, then RUN.
            4. If the program does not start, check the machine model it requires: 48K or 128K.
        """,
    ),
)

        summary = "A simple language for beginners: in the 80s almost all home computers were programmed in it.",
        details = "Kemeny and Kurtz created BASIC at Dartmouth College. Dialects were in the ROM of the ZX Spectrum, the Commodore 64 " +
            "and the Apple II; BASICA came with the IBM PC, and in the USSR it was in the BK-0010.",
        example = """
            10 PRINT "HELLO"
            20 GOTO 10
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.LANGUAGE,
        section = "Educational and exotic",
        title = "Logo",
        originalTitle = "Logo",
        author = "Wally Feurzeig, Seymour Papert, Cynthia Solomon",
        year = 1967,
        summary = "A turtle language: commands move an on-screen turtle and draw patterns.",
        details = "Logo was created to teach children programming. Papert described the ideas in the book “Mindstorms” (1980): " +

        example = """
            REPEAT 4 [FORWARD 100 RIGHT 90]
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.LANGUAGE,
        section = "Educational and exotic",
        title = "Forth",
        originalTitle = "Forth",
        author = "Charles Moore",
        year = 1970,
        summary = "A stack language: few keywords, compact code, fast work on weak machines.",
        details = "Moore created Forth in the late 1960s for controlling telescopes. A program is a dictionary of words, " +
            "and each word works with the stack. In the 80s Forth was valued in robotics and control, where memory and speed " +

        example = """
            : SQUARE  DUP * ;

        """,

            "with mathematical symbols and wrote very short code.",

            +/⍳10   ⍝ sum of the numbers from 1 to 10: 55
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.LANGUAGE,

            "so a search in text is written briefly.",

            every write(find("a", "banana"))   # prints 2, 4 and 6

    entry(

        year = 1977,

        year = 1979,

        example = """

              else

            end;
        """,

        details = "The convention was proposed by Charles Simonyi, who worked at Xerox PARC and then at Microsoft. " +

            "The minus: when the type changes, the prefix starts lying.",

        details = "Meyer introduced the approach together with the Eiffel language (the first version, 1986). A contract divides responsibility: " +

        example = """

    // ---------- Optimization and memory ----------

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Optimization and memory",
        title = "Cycle-level optimization",
        originalTitle = "Cycle-level optimization",
        author = "Game and demoscene programmers",
        year = 1981,
        summary = "Critical sections are written in assembler and counted in cycles: every processor cycle mattered.",
        details = "In the 80s games for the IBM PC, ZX Spectrum, Commodore 64 and Amiga were written in assembler. Typical techniques: " +
            "unrolled loops, tables instead of computation, direct access to video memory, and string instructions that do the work in one pass.",
        example = """
            ; Clearing the 80x25 text screen: 2000 cells of 2 bytes each
            mov ax, 0B800h        ; video memory segment (colour text)
            mov es, ax
            xor di, di            ; offset 0
            mov ax, 0720h         ; 07 — attribute (white on black), 20 — space
            mov cx, 2000          ; number of words
            rep stosw             ; store AX into ES:DI, CX times
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Optimization and memory",
        title = "Fixed-point arithmetic",
        originalTitle = "Fixed-point arithmetic",
        author = "Practice of the 80s",
        year = 1980,
        summary = "Fractions are stored as integers with an implicit scale: the coprocessor was expensive and not in every PC.",
        details = "The Intel 8087 coprocessor (1980) was expensive and not in every computer. Fractional values were written as integers: " +
            "the high bits are the integer part, the low bits the fraction. Addition stays ordinary, and after multiplication the result is shifted.",
        example = """
            #include <stdio.h>

            typedef int fix8;                  /* 8.8 format: the high 8 bits are the integer part */
            #define FX_ONE 256                 /* 1.0 */
            #define FX_MUL(a, b) (((long)(a) * (b)) >> 8)

            int main(void)
            {
                fix8 x = 384;                  /* 1.5 */
                fix8 y = FX_MUL(x, x);         /* 2.25, that is 576 */

                printf("%d\n", y);
                return 0;
            }
        """,
    ),

        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,

        year = 1985,
        summary = "A frame is drawn in an invisible buffer and shown whole: no flicker and no torn picture.",
        details = "The Amiga and the Atari ST (1985), as well as the EGA and VGA video modes, could hold several video pages. " +
            "While one page is shown, the next frame is drawn in the other, and then the video memory pointer is switched.",
    ),

    entry(

        title = "Overlays",
        originalTitle = "Overlays",
        author = "MS-DOS practice",
        year = 1981,
        summary = "A program is bigger than the available memory: its parts are loaded from disk in turn into the same place.",
        details = "MS-DOS addressed 640 KB, and large programs — compilers, CAD systems, editors — did not fit into it. " +
            "They were split into overlays: only the current part lay in memory. Turbo Pascal had a ready-made Overlay module for this.",
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Optimization and memory",

        originalTitle = "Garbage collection",
        author = "John McCarthy",
        year = 1958,
        summary = "The environment itself finds and frees memory that nothing refers to any more.",
        details = "The garbage collector was invented for Lisp (1958). In the 80s it was used by Smalltalk and Lisp machines, while PC programmers " +
            "called malloc and free by hand and often made mistakes: memory leaks and dangling pointers were the plague of the era.",
    ),

    // ---------- Data and storage ----------

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Data and storage",
        title = "Relational model",
        originalTitle = "Relational model",
        author = "Edgar F. Codd",
        year = 1970,
        summary = "Data lives in tables, links are expressed by key values, and a query describes the desired result.",
        details = "Codd's article “A Relational Model of Data for Large Shared Data Banks” (1970) separated the logic of data from the way it is stored. " +
            "In the 80s relational DBMSs displaced hierarchical and network databases: design became simpler and queries more flexible.",
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PRACTICE,
        section = "Data and storage",
        title = "Entity-relationship model",
        originalTitle = "Entity-Relationship model",
        author = "Peter Pin-Shan Chen",
        year = 1976,
        summary = "A subject area is described by entities, their attributes and relationships, and then translated into tables.",
        details = "Chen's article “The Entity-Relationship Model — Toward a Unified View of Data” (1976) set the language of database design. " +
            "An ER diagram on paper is a short path to a table schema.",

        example = """
            -- Bad: the client's address is repeated in every order
            CREATE TABLE orders_flat (
                order_id       INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
                client_name    TEXT,
                client_address TEXT,
                item           TEXT
            );

            -- Better: the client is stored only once
            CREATE TABLE clients (
                client_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
                name      TEXT,
                address   TEXT
            );

            CREATE TABLE orders (
                order_id  INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
                client_id INTEGER REFERENCES clients (client_id),

            );

package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.Kind
import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/** Built products: finished programs with an explanation of how they work and instructions for running them. */
val db1Projects: List<RecordEntity> = listOf(

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PROJECT,
        section = "Games and simulations",
        title = "Game of Life (Conway) on awk",
        originalTitle = "Conway's Game of Life in awk",
        author = "John Conway (rules), implementation in awk",
        year = 1970,
        summary = "A cellular automaton in the terminal: generations are born, die and move according to Conway's rules.",
        details = "A cell survives if 2 or 3 neighbours are alive, and is born if exactly 3 neighbours are alive. " +
            "The field wraps around into a torus: the edge leads to the opposite edge. " +
            "Live cells are stored in the associative array cell, keyed by “row, column”. " +
            "At each step the program builds a new field nxt and copies it back. The glider, five cells, moves diagonally.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as life.awk.
            2. Run: awk -f life.awk
            3. To see more generations, increase GENS in the BEGIN block. Add new shapes in the same block: cell[row, column] = 1.
        """,
        example = """
            # Conway's Game of Life. Run: awk -f life.awk
            BEGIN {
                W = 20; H = 10; GENS = 5
                # starting shape: a glider
                cell[1, 2] = 1; cell[2, 3] = 1
                cell[3, 1] = 1; cell[3, 2] = 1; cell[3, 3] = 1
                for (g = 0; g < GENS; g++) {
                    show()
                    step()
                }
            }

            function show(   y, x, line, ch) {
                for (y = 0; y < H; y++) {
                    line = ""
                    for (x = 0; x < W; x++) {
                        ch = "."
                        if ((y, x) in cell) ch = "#"
                        line = line ch
                    }
                    print line
                }
                print "----"
            }

            function step(   y, x, dy, dx, n, ny, nx, alive, nxt, k) {
                delete nxt
                for (y = 0; y < H; y++)
                    for (x = 0; x < W; x++) {
                        n = 0
                        for (dy = -1; dy <= 1; dy++)
                            for (dx = -1; dx <= 1; dx++) {
                                if (dy == 0 && dx == 0) continue
                                ny = (y + dy + H) % H
                                nx = (x + dx + W) % W
                                if ((ny, nx) in cell) n++
                            }
                        alive = ((y, x) in cell)
                        if ((alive && (n == 2 || n == 3)) || (!alive && n == 3))
                            nxt[y, x] = 1
                    }
                delete cell
                for (k in nxt) cell[k] = 1
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PROJECT,
        section = "Games and simulations",
        title = "Random maze in the console",
        originalTitle = "Maze generator with depth-first backtracking",
        author = "80s practice, depth-first search with backtracking",
        year = 1980,
        summary = "The program carves passages through a grid of walls: the result is a tree without cycles, where there is exactly one path between any two points.",
        details = "Depth-first search with backtracking: from the current cell we choose a random neighbour that has not been visited yet, " +
            "knock down the wall between them and continue. If there are no neighbours left, we backtrack. " +
            "Cells sit at odd positions of the grid and walls at even positions, so the structure is always valid.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as maze.c and build it: cc -o maze maze.c
            2. Run it several times: each run gives a new maze, because the generator depends on the time.
            3. To make the maze bigger, increase N (the number of cells along one side). N must be odd.
        """,
        example = """
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include <stdlib.h>
            #include <time.h>

            #define N 11                     /* number of cells along one side */
            #define W (2 * N + 1)            /* full grid: cells and the walls between them */

            static char grid[W][W];
            static int visited[N][N];

            static void carve(int cy, int cx)
            {
                static const int dy[4] = { -1, 1, 0, 0 };
                static const int dx[4] = { 0, 0, -1, 1 };
                int order[4] = { 0, 1, 2, 3 };
                int i, j, tmp;

                visited[cy][cx] = 1;
                grid[2 * cy + 1][2 * cx + 1] = ' ';

                for (i = 3; i > 0; i--) {    /* shuffle the directions */
                    j = rand() % (i + 1);
                    tmp = order[i];
                    order[i] = order[j];
                    order[j] = tmp;
                }

                for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
                    int d = order[i];
                    int ny = cy + dy[d];
                    int nx = cx + dx[d];

                    if (ny < 0 || ny >= N || nx < 0 || nx >= N || visited[ny][nx])
                        continue;
                    grid[2 * cy + 1 + dy[d]][2 * cx + 1 + dx[d]] = ' ';   /* knock down the wall */
                    carve(ny, nx);
                }
            }

            int main(void)
            {
                int y, x;

                srand((unsigned)time(NULL));
                for (y = 0; y < W; y++)
                    for (x = 0; x < W; x++)
                        grid[y][x] = '#';

                carve(0, 0);

                for (y = 0; y < W; y++) {
                    for (x = 0; x < W; x++)
                        putchar(grid[y][x]);
                    putchar('\n');
                }
                return 0;
            }
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PROJECT,
        section = "Utilities",
        title = "Address book in sh",
        originalTitle = "Address book in plain sh and awk",
        author = "Shell script practice, 1980s",
        year = 1979,
        summary = "A phone book in one script: add a record, find one, show everything. No database is needed.",
        details = "The records live in a text file, book.txt: one record per line, fields separated by tabs. " +
            "The add and find commands are branches of the shell that call printf, grep and awk. " +
            "The project shows that much of what a database does can be done with a text file in a clear format and a couple of Unix tools.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the script as book.sh and make it executable: chmod +x book.sh
            2. Add records: ./book.sh add Ivan 555-1234 and ./book.sh add Maria 555-9876
            3. Find a record: ./book.sh find Ivan
            4. Show all records: ./book.sh list
        """,
        example = """
            #!/bin/sh
            # Address book: records are stored in book.txt, fields are separated by tabs
            case "$1" in
              add)  printf '%s\t%s\n' "$2" "$3" >> book.txt ;;
              find) grep -i "$2" book.txt ;;
              list) awk -F '\t' '{ printf "%-15s %s\n", $1, $2 }' book.txt ;;
              *)    echo "usage: book.sh add|find|list" ;;
            esac
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.PROJECT,
        section = "Development tools",
        title = "Calculator with yacc",
        originalTitle = "Calculator with yacc grammar and a hand-written lexer",
        author = "Stephen Johnson (yacc, 1975), Unix practice",
        year = 1975,
        summary = "A grammar instead of hand-written parsing: yacc builds a parser from rules, and precedence is set by the declaration lines.",
        details = "The file has three parts: declarations (tokens and operator precedence), grammar rules with actions written in C, and the lexer code. " +
            "You do not code the precedence of * over + by hand: it is set by the order of the %left lines. " +
            "The action $$ = $1 + $3 computes the value of the expression. " +
            "This is how calculators, configurators and compilers were written in the 80s.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the grammar as calc.y. The lexer is in the third part of the file; you do not need to change it.
            2. Generate the parser: bison -y -d calc.y (this creates y.tab.c and y.tab.h).
            3. Build: cc -o calc y.tab.c
            4. Run: echo "2 + 3 * (4 - 1)" | ./calc — the answer is 11.
        """,
        example = """
            %{
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include <ctype.h>
            int yylex(void);
            void yyerror(const char *s);
            %}

            %union {
                double val;
            }

            %token <val> NUMBER
            %type <val> expr
            %left '+' '-'
            %left '*' '/'
            %right UMINUS

            %%
            line : /* empty */
                 | line expr '\n'         { printf("%g\n", $2); }
                 ;
            expr : expr '+' expr          { $$ = $1 + $3; }
                 | expr '-' expr          { $$ = $1 - $3; }
                 | expr '*' expr          { $$ = $1 * $3; }
                 | expr '/' expr          { $$ = $1 / $3; }
                 | '-' expr %prec UMINUS  { $$ = -$2; }
                 | '(' expr ')'           { $$ = $2; }
                 | NUMBER                 { $$ = $1; }
                 ;
            %%

            int yylex(void)
            {
                int c;

                while ((c = getchar()) == ' ' || c == '\t')
                    ;
                if (isdigit(c) || c == '.') {
                    ungetc(c, stdin);
                    scanf("%lf", &yylval.val);
                    return NUMBER;
                }
                return c;
            }

            void yyerror(const char *s)
            {
                fprintf(stderr, "%s\n", s);
            }

            int main(void)
            {
                return yyparse();
            }
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),
)

package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.Kind
import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/** Tools of the 1980s: build, compilers, editors, debugging, typesetting and the command line. */
val db1Tools: List<RecordEntity> = listOf(

    // ---------- Build and analysis ----------

    entry(
        kind = Kind.TOOL,
        section = "Build and analysis",
        title = "The make utility",
        originalTitle = "make",
        author = "Stuart Feldman, Bell Labs",
        year = 1976,
        summary = "A recipe file describes which files depend on which and how to build them. Only what has changed is rebuilt.",
        details = "The make utility appeared at Bell Labs in 1976. It compares the modification times of files and runs only the commands that are needed. " +
            "In the 80s the Makefile was the standard for C projects on Unix, and many projects still build this way.",
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.TOOL,
        section = "Build and analysis",
        title = "The lint static analyzer",
        originalTitle = "lint",
        author = "Stephen Johnson, Bell Labs",
        year = 1978,
        summary = "Checks C code before it runs and finds suspicious spots that the compiler lets through.",
        details = "Lint caught mismatched argument types, variables used before assignment, and code that could never run. " +
            "In the 80s it was run before a release. It is a direct ancestor of modern static analyzers.",
        example = """
            int average(a, n)
            int *a;
            int n;
            {
                int sum, i;
                for (i = 0; i < n; i++)
                    sum += a[i];       /* lint: sum is used before it is assigned */
                return sum / n;
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        kind = Kind.TOOL,
        section = "Build and analysis",
        title = "The gprof profiler",
        originalTitle = "gprof",
        author = "Graham, Kessler, McKusick (Berkeley)",
        year = 1982,
        summary = "Shows how much time functions take and who calls whom.",
        details = "gprof built a call graph and distributed the running time among the functions. " +
            "Knuth's phrase “premature optimization is the root of all evil” (1974) is about exactly this kind of measurement.",
    ),

# FILE: RetroDatabases/app/src/main/java/com/example/retrodatabases/data/seed/Db2Cross.kt

package com.example.retrodatabases.data.seed

import com.example.retrodatabases.data.Kind
import com.example.retrodatabases.data.RecordEntity

/** Cross-code experiments of the 1990s: technologies mixed on purpose, to see what happens. */
val db2Cross: List<RecordEntity> = listOf(

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Java and native code",
        title = "Java calls C through JNI",
        originalTitle = "Java Native Interface",
        author = "Sun Microsystems and the JNI team",
        year = 1997,
        summary = "A Java program calls a function written in C, which is how Java programs reused old libraries.",
        details = "The Java Native Interface (JNI), part of JDK 1.1 in 1997, lets a class declare a native method. " +
            "The C side implements a function with a generated name, and System.loadLibrary loads the shared library at start-up. " +
            "The Java code stays portable; only the library is built for each platform.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the two files below in one folder. Compile the Java part and generate the C header: javac -h . Native.java
            2. Build the shared library from the C file: cc -shared -fPIC -I"<DOLLAR>JAVA_HOME/include" -I"<DOLLAR>JAVA_HOME/include/linux" native.c -o libnative.so
            3. Run the Java program and point it at the library: java -Djava.library.path=. Native
        """,
        example = """
            ### Native.java
            public class Native {
                static native int add(int a, int b);

                static {
                    System.loadLibrary("native");
                }

                public static void main(String[] args) {
                    System.out.println("2 + 3 = " + add(2, 3));
                }
            }
            ### native.c
            #include <jni.h>
            #include "Native.h"

            JNIEXPORT jint JNICALL Java_Native_add(JNIEnv *env, jclass cls, jint a, jint b)
            {
                return a + b;
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Web and processes",
        title = "PHP runs a C program",
        originalTitle = "exec() from a PHP page",
        author = "CGI and PHP practice",
        year = 1997,
        summary = "A PHP page calls a compiled C program through the shell and shows its output.",
        details = "Server-side scripts of the time could start other programs with exec(). " +
            "The page passes an argument to a small C program, which does the arithmetic, and PHP puts the result into the HTML. " +
            "The trade-off was speed against safety: anyone who could influence the arguments could run commands.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the two files below in one folder and compile the C program: cc -o square square.c
            2. Run the page from the command line: php -f page.php
            3. On a web server the same code works if the server allows the script to run the program.
        """,
        example = """
            ### square.c
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include <stdlib.h>

            int main(int argc, char *argv[])
            {
                int n = argc > 1 ? atoi(argv[1]) : 10;
                printf("%d\n", n * n);
                return 0;
            }
            ### page.php
            <?php
            <DOLLAR>n = 7;
            exec("./square <DOLLAR>n", <DOLLAR>output);
            echo "The square of <DOLLAR>n is <DOLLAR>output[0]\n";
            ?>
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Build-time generation",
        title = "Perl writes a C table at build time",
        originalTitle = "Generated header",
        author = "Makefile and Perl practice",
        year = 1995,
        summary = "A Perl script writes a C header with a table of squares, and the C program includes it when it is built.",
        details = "Code generators were common in the 90s: a script produced a header or a table from a specification, and the build compiled the result. " +
            "Here Perl writes the array and the C program only reads it. Changing the script changes the table without touching the C code.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the two files below in one folder.
            2. Generate the header and build the program: perl gen.pl > table.h, then cc -o main main.c
            3. Run it: ./main prints 1, 4, 9, 16 and 25.
        """,
        example = """
            ### gen.pl
            #!/usr/bin/perl
            # Writes a C array of squares to standard output
            print "static const int squares[] = {\n";
            for my <DOLLAR>i (1 .. 5) {
                print "    <DOLLAR>i * <DOLLAR>i,\n";
            }
            print "};\n";
            print "static const int count = 5;\n";
            ### main.c
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include "table.h"

            int main(void)
            {
                int i;
                for (i = 0; i < count; i++)
                    printf("%d\n", squares[i]);
                return 0;
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Embedding",
        title = "Lua embedded in a C program",
        originalTitle = "Embedding Lua",
        author = "PUC-Rio Lua team",
        year = 1996,
        summary = "A C host creates a Lua state and runs a script, so the game logic can change without recompiling the engine.",
        details = "Lua was designed to live inside other programs. The host creates an interpreter state, opens the standard libraries and runs script text or files. " +
            "Game engines of the late 90s used this pattern to keep level and weapon logic in scripts. " +
            "The example uses the function names of later Lua versions, but the idea is the one the 90s engines used.",
        instructions = """
            1. Install Lua with its development headers.
            2. Build the host against the Lua library: cc -o host host.c -llua -lm
            3. Run ./host: the embedded script prints one line.
        """,
        example = """
            #include <stdio.h>
            #include "lua.h"
            #include "lauxlib.h"
            #include "lualib.h"

            int main(void)
            {
                lua_State *L = luaL_newstate();
                luaL_openlibs(L);
                if (luaL_dostring(L, "print('the game logic runs in Lua')") != 0)
                    fprintf(stderr, "%s\n", lua_tostring(L, -1));
                lua_close(L);
                return 0;
            }
        """,
        rare = true,
    ),

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
        kind = Kind.CROSS,
        section = "Windows API",
        title = "Visual Basic calls a Windows DLL",
        originalTitle = "Declare statement",
        author = "Microsoft Visual Basic practice",
        year = 1995,
        summary = "A Visual Basic form calls MessageBox from user32.dll through a Declare statement.",
        details = "Visual Basic could reach any function in a Windows DLL with a Declare statement, which described the function's parameters. " +
            "The program was a form in Visual Basic, and the Windows API did the rest. " +
            "This was how VB programs of the decade used system services that the language itself did not offer.",
        instructions = """
            1. Create a form with a button named cmdHello.
            2. Paste the declaration and the handler into the form's code window.
            3. Run the program and click the button: a Windows message box appears.
        """,
        example = """
            Private Declare Function MessageBox Lib "user32" Alias "MessageBoxA" _
                (ByVal hWnd As Long, ByVal lpText As String, ByVal lpCaption As String, ByVal wType As Long) As Long

            Private Sub cmdHello_Click()
                MessageBox 0, "Hello from a Visual Basic form", "Cross-code", 0
            End Sub
        """,
    ),
)

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
        kind = Kind.EXAMPLE,
        section = "Files and state",
        title = "Visit counter in a file",
        originalTitle = "Counter kept in counter.txt",
        author = "Common CGI practice",
        year = 1996,
        summary = "A page counter that keeps its value in a text file, so it survives between runs.",
        details = "Without a database, the usual way to keep state on a 90s web server was a text file. " +
            "The script reads the number, adds one and writes the new value back. The same trick worked for guest books and polls.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as counter.pl.
            2. Run it: perl counter.pl
            3. Run it again: the number goes up on every run.
            4. Delete counter.txt to start from zero.
        """,
        example = """
            #!/usr/bin/perl
            # Keeps the visit count in counter.txt
            my <DOLLAR>file = 'counter.txt';
            my <DOLLAR>count = 0;
            if (open(my <DOLLAR>in, '<', <DOLLAR>file)) {
                <DOLLAR>count = <<DOLLAR>in> + 0;
                close(<DOLLAR>in);
            }
            <DOLLAR>count++;
            open(my <DOLLAR>out, '>', <DOLLAR>file) or die "cannot write <DOLLAR>file: <DOLLAR>!";
            print <DOLLAR>out "<DOLLAR>count\n";
            close(<DOLLAR>out);
            print "Visit number <DOLLAR>count\n";
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
        kind = Kind.EXAMPLE,
        section = "Text and collections",
        title = "Word count with Hashtable",
        originalTitle = "Hashtable word count (Java 1.0 style)",
        author = "Java 1.0 practice",
        year = 1996,
        summary = "Counting words with java.util.Hashtable in a console program, the way early Java programs did.",
        details = "The Hashtable class holds the counts, keyed by word. The program reads standard input line by line and splits each line into words with StringTokenizer. " +
            "Java 1.0 had no generics and no autoboxing, so the counts were wrapped in Integer objects by hand.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the example as WordCount.java.
            2. Compile it: javac WordCount.java
            3. Run it with some text: echo "the cat and the hat" | java WordCount
        """,
        example = """
            import java.io.*;
            import java.util.*;

            public class WordCount {
                public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {
                    Hashtable counts = new Hashtable();
                    BufferedReader in = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(System.in));
                    String line;
                    while ((line = in.readLine()) != null) {
                        StringTokenizer words = new StringTokenizer(line);
                        while (words.hasMoreTokens()) {
                            String w = words.nextToken();
                            Integer n = (Integer) counts.get(w);
                            counts.put(w, new Integer(n == null ? 1 : n.intValue() + 1));
                        }
                    }
                    Enumeration keys = counts.keys();
                    while (keys.hasMoreElements()) {
                        String w = (String) keys.nextElement();
                        System.out.println(w + " " + counts.get(w));
                    }
                }
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,

        author = "C++98 practice",
        year = 1998,
        summary = "A small class with operator() tells std::sort how to order the words.",
        details = "std::sort takes a comparison object as its third argument. The object is a class with operator(), which the algorithm calls for each pair of elements. " +
            "Because the object carries its own type, the compiler can inline the comparison, so the code runs as fast as a hand-written loop.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the example as sortwords.cpp.
            2. Compile it in C++98 mode: g++ -std=c++98 -o sortwords sortwords.cpp
            3. Run ./sortwords: the words come out from the shortest to the longest.
        """,
        example = """
            #include <algorithm>
            #include <iostream>
            #include <string>
            #include <vector>

            struct ByLength {
                bool operator()(const std::string& a, const std::string& b) const {
                    return a.size() < b.size();
                }
            };

            int main() {
                std::vector<std::string> words;
                words.push_back("compiler");
                words.push_back("c");
                words.push_back("linker");
                std::sort(words.begin(), words.end(), ByLength());
                for (std::size_t i = 0; i < words.size(); ++i)
                    std::cout << words[i] << "\n";
                return 0;
            }
        """,

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
        kind = Kind.FLEX,
        section = "Graphics and maths",
        title = "Mandelbrot set in plain JavaScript",
        originalTitle = "Mandelbrot in ASCII",
        author = "Demo-style showpiece",
        year = 1995,
        summary = "About twenty lines of JavaScript draw the Mandelbrot set in the console, as ASCII.",
        details = "The loop iterates z = z * z + c for each character cell. A cell gets a dot, a plus or a hash, depending on how long the value stays bounded. " +
            "The code uses only the basic features that 1990s browsers supported, so it would have run in a page of the decade.",
        instructions = """
            1. Save the code as mandel.js.
            2. Run it: node mandel.js
            3. Make the picture larger or move it by changing the numbers in x / 30 and y / 12.
        """,
        example = """
            for (var y = -12; y <= 12; y++) {
                var line = "";
                for (var x = -39; x <= 20; x++) {
                    var cr = x / 30, ci = y / 12;
                    var zr = 0, zi = 0, n = 0;
                    while (n < 50 && zr * zr + zi * zi < 4) {
                        var t = zr * zr - zi * zi + cr;
                        zi = 2 * zr * zi + ci;
                        zr = t;
                        n++;
                    }
                    line += n == 50 ? "#" : (n > 5 ? "+" : ".");
                }
                console.log(line);
            }
        """,
    ),

    entry(
        databaseId = 2,
